2. Let *m* be ? LookupSpecial(*x*, `"__float__"`). If *m* is not **absent**:
   let *r* be ? Call(*m*, «»); if Type(*r*) is `float`, return it; if a strict
   subclass, issue a `DeprecationWarning` and return a `float` with its value;
   otherwise raise a `TypeError` exception ("*fqname*(*x*).`__float__`() must
   return a float, not *tname*(*r*)").
3. If LookupSpecial(*x*, `"__index__"`) is not **absent**, return
   IntToFloat(? ToIndex(*x*)).
4. Raise a `TypeError` exception ("float() argument must be a string or a real
   number, not '*tname*(*x*)'").

StringToFloat strips white space and accepts an optional sign followed by
`inf`, `infinity`, or `nan` in any case, or by a decimal number as the
`floatnumber` or `intpart` of 12.6 (digits may be any Nd characters, single
underscores between digits). The value is the correctly rounded `float`;
magnitudes too large are infinities and too small zeros. Other text raises a
`ValueError` exception ("could not convert string to float: *r*") with *r* the
repr of the argument.

The class method `from_number(x)` performs steps 2–4 for a non-string *x*, and
raises a `TypeError` exception ("must be real number, not *tname*(*x*)") for a
string.

#### 22.3.2 Arithmetic

`+`, `-`, `*`, and `/` are the IEEE 754 operations. Division by a zero raises a
`ZeroDivisionError` exception ("division by zero"), as do `//`, `%`, and
`divmod`. FloatDivMod(*x*, *y*) gives `//` and `%`:

1. Let *mod* be the IEEE remainder fmod(*x*, *y*) (the sign of *x*, exact).
2. Let *div* be (*x* − *mod*) / *y*.
3. If *mod* is not zero and its sign differs from that of *y*, set *mod* to
   *mod* + *y* and *div* to *div* − 1. If *mod* is zero, give it the sign of
   *y*.
4. If *div* is not zero, let *fd* be ⌊*div*⌋, plus 1 if *div* − *fd* > 0.5;
   otherwise let *fd* be a zero with the sign of *x* / *y*.
5. Return (*fd*, *mod*).

`float.__pow__(x, y)`:

1. If *x* is zero and *y* < 0, raise a `ZeroDivisionError` exception ("zero to
   a negative power").
2. If *x* < 0 and *y* is finite and not an integer, return the `complex` power
   of (*x* + 0j) and *y* (22.4.2).
3. Otherwise return the IEEE 754 pow(*x*, *y*) with its special cases (1 to any
   power and any number to the power 0 are 1.0; (−1) ** ±∞ is 1.0). If the
   result is infinite while both operands are finite, raise an
   `OverflowError` exception whose `args` are (ERANGE, `"Result too large"`),
   ERANGE being the host's error number (34 on POSIX hosts).

A modulus is not accepted: `pow(x, y, m)` with a `float` operand raises a
`TypeError` exception ("pow() 3rd argument not allowed unless all arguments
are integers"). Bitwise operators and shifts are not defined for `float`.

#### 22.3.3 Comparison

Floats compare as IEEE 754 values: a NaN is unequal to everything, itself
included, and unordered; −0.0 equals 0.0. Comparison with an `int` is exact
(22.1.4).

#### 22.3.4 FloatRepr ( *x* )

`repr(x)` and `str(x)` return FloatRepr(*x*):

1. If *x* is a NaN, return `"nan"`; if an infinity, `"inf"` or `"-inf"`.
2. Let *d* be the shortest string of decimal digits *d*₁…*d*ₖ, and *e* the
   integer, such that *d*₁ ≠ 0 (or *k* = 1 and *d* is `"0"`) and the decimal
   value 0.*d*₁…*d*ₖ × 10<sup>*e*</sup> rounds to |*x*|; among the shortest,
   choose the one nearest to |*x*|.
3. Let *s* be `"-"` if *x* has its sign bit set and `""` otherwise.
4. If −4 < *e* ≤ 16, return *s* followed by *d* written in positional notation
   with the decimal point after *e* digits (padding with zeros), and with
   `".0"` appended when there is no fractional digit.
5. Otherwise return *s* followed by *d*₁, then `"."` and *d*₂…*d*ₖ if *k* > 1,
   then `"e"`, the sign of *e* − 1 (`+` or `-`), and |*e* − 1| with at least
   two digits.

#### 22.3.5 Methods

| Method | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `is_integer()` | whether *x* is finite and integral |
| `as_integer_ratio()` | the pair of integers (*m*, *n*) in lowest terms with *n* > 0 and *m* / *n* = *x* exactly; an infinity raises `OverflowError` ("cannot convert Infinity to integer ratio"), a NaN `ValueError` ("cannot convert NaN to integer ratio") |
| `hex()` | `"[-]0x0.0p+0"` for a zero; otherwise `"[-]0x"` followed by the leading digit (`1` for normal numbers, `0` for subnormals), `"."`, the 13 hexadecimal fraction digits, `"p"`, and the signed decimal binary exponent (−1022 for subnormals); `"inf"`, `"-inf"`, `"nan"` for the special values |
| `fromhex(s)` (class method) | the value of the text: optional white space and sign, `inf`/`infinity`/`nan`, or an optional `0x` prefix, hexadecimal digits with an optional point, and an optional `p` exponent, rounded correctly; other text raises `ValueError` ("invalid hexadecimal floating-point string"), a value too large `OverflowError` ("hexadecimal value too large to represent as a float") |
| `conjugate()`, `real` | *x*; `imag` 0.0 |
| `__trunc__()`, `__int__()` | 22.1.1 |
| `__floor__()`, `__ceil__()` | ⌊*x*⌋, ⌈*x*⌉ as `int`, with the errors of 22.1.1 |
| `__round__(ndigits=None)` | 22.3.6 |

#### 22.3.6 Rounding

`float.__round__(ndigits)`:

1. If *ndigits* is `None`: return the integer nearest to *x*, ties to even, as
   an `int`; an infinity or NaN raises the error of 22.1.1.
2. Let *n* be ℝ(? ToIndex(*ndigits*)). If *x* is not finite or zero, return
   *x*.
3. Let *r* be the multiple of 10<sup>−*n*</sup> nearest to the exact value of
   *x*, ties to even, and return the `float` nearest to *r*. (Because the exact
   value of the float 2.675 is below 2.675, `round(2.675, 2)` is 2.67.) If that
   is an infinity, raise an `OverflowError` exception ("rounded value too large
   to represent").

### 22.4 The `complex` type

A `complex` value is a pair of `float` values, its real and imaginary parts,
read-only as `real` and `imag` ("readonly attribute").

#### 22.4.1 complex ( *real*=0, *imag*=0 )

1. If *real* is a `str`: if *imag* is given, raise a `TypeError` exception
   ("complex() argument 'real' must be a real number, not str"); otherwise
   return ? StringToComplex(*real*).
2. If *imag* is a `str`, raise a `TypeError` exception ("complex() argument
   'imag' must be a real number, not str").
3. Let *r* be ? ToComplex(*real*) (below); if *real* is not a number, raise a
   `TypeError` exception ("complex() argument must be a string or a number,
   not *tname*(*real*)").
4. If *imag* is not given, return *r*. Otherwise let *i* be ? ToComplex(*imag*)
   and return the complex number with real part *r*.real − *i*.imag and
   imaginary part *r*.imag + *i*.real.

ToComplex(*o*): if *o* is a `complex`, its value; else call `__complex__` if
present, requiring a `complex` result ("*fqname*(*o*).`__complex__`() must
return a complex, not *tname*(*r*)"); else the `float` conversion of 22.3.1
steps 2–3 with an imaginary part 0.0.

StringToComplex strips white space and accepts, optionally inside one pair of
parentheses (with white space inside them), a real number, an imaginary number
(a real number or nothing, followed by `j` or `J`), or a real number followed
by a signed imaginary number, each real number written as for StringToFloat.
Other text raises a `ValueError` exception ("complex() arg is a malformed
string").

`complex.from_number(x)` is ToComplex(*x*), refusing strings as
`float.from_number` does.

#### 22.4.2 Arithmetic

For *a* = *p* + *q*j and *b* = *r* + *s*j:

- `a + b` and `a - b` are componentwise.
- `a * b` is (*pr* − *qs*) + (*ps* + *qr*)j, computed in IEEE 754 arithmetic
  after any conversion of a real operand to `complex` (so `inf * (1+0j)` is
  `(inf+nanj)`).
- `a / b` raises a `ZeroDivisionError` exception ("division by zero") if *b* is
  0, and otherwise is computed by Smith's algorithm, scaling by whichever of
  |*r*| and |*s*| is larger.
- `a ** b`: if *b* has no imaginary part and is an integer *n* with |*n*| ≤
  100, the result is *a*\*\*|*n*| computed by repeated squaring with the
  products of `*`, and for *n* ≤ 0 the quotient 1 / that power, where a zero
  power raises a `ZeroDivisionError` exception ("zero to a negative or complex
  power"). Otherwise, if *a* is 0 the result is 0j, unless *b*.imag ≠ 0 or
  *b*.real < 0, which raise that `ZeroDivisionError`; and otherwise the result
  is exp(*b* × log *a*) in polar form. If either part of the result is
  infinite, whatever the operands, raise an `OverflowError` exception
  ("complex exponentiation").
- `-a`, `+a`, `abs(a)` (the hypotenuse, `OverflowError` "absolute value too
  large" when it overflows), and `conjugate()`.

`//`, `%`, `divmod`, the ordering comparisons, and the bitwise operators are not
defined for `complex`. `pow` with a third argument raises a `ValueError`
exception ("complex modulo"). `==` compares both parts, and a `complex` equals
an `int` or `float` when its imaginary part is zero and its real part equals it.

#### 22.4.3 ComplexRepr ( *z* )

Let *fmt*(*v*) be FloatRepr(*v*) without the trailing `".0"` added by step 4.
If *z*.real is +0.0, return *fmt*(*z*.imag) followed by `"j"`. Otherwise return
`"("`, *fmt*(*z*.real), the sign of *z*.imag (`+` unless its sign bit is set;
a NaN is written with `+`), *fmt*(|*z*.imag|), and `"j)"`.

## 23 Text Processing

### 23.1 The `str` type

A `str` value is an immutable finite sequence of code points, each an integer
in 0 through 0x10FFFF (lone surrogates included). Indexing a string yields a
string of length 1; there is no separate character type. String comparison
(`<`, `==`, …) is lexicographic by code point value. The hash of a string is
implementation-defined but is the same for equal strings within one run.

#### 23.1.1 str ( *object*=`''` ) and str ( *object*=`b''`, *encoding*=`'utf-8'`, *errors*=`'strict'` )

1. If neither *encoding* nor *errors* is given, return ? ToStr(*object*)
   (7.5.5), converted to an exact `str` when the class called is `str`.
2. If *object* is a `str`, raise a `TypeError` exception ("decoding str is not
   supported"). If it does not support the buffer protocol, raise a `TypeError`
   exception ("decoding to str: need a bytes-like object, *tname*(*object*)
   found").
3. Return ? Decode(the bytes of *object*, *encoding*, *errors*) (23.3).

At most three arguments are accepted ("str expected at most 3 arguments, got
*n*"), with keywords `object`, `encoding`, and `errors`.

When the class called is a strict subclass *C* of `str`, the result is a new
instance of *C* with the code points computed above. `str.__new__(T)` for a
*T* that is not a subtype of `str` raises a `TypeError` exception
("str.\_\_new\_\_(*T*): *T* is not a subtype of str").

#### 23.1.2 Sequence operations

Strings support the common sequence operations (25.5) with these specific
messages: an index out of range raises `IndexError` ("string index out of
range"); a non-integer index `TypeError` ("string indices must be integers,
not '*tname*(*i*)'"); `s + t` with a non-string *t* `TypeError` ("can only
concatenate str (not "*tname*(*t*)") to str"). `x in s` requires a string *x*
("'in \<string\>' requires string as left operand, not *tname*(*x*)") and is
true when *x* occurs in *s* as a contiguous substring (the empty string occurs
in every string).

#### 23.1.3 StrRepr ( *s* )

1. Let *q* be `'` unless *s* contains `'` and no `"`, in which case let *q* be
   `"`.
2. Let *r* be *q*. For each code point *c* of *s*: append `\\` for a
   backslash, `\` *q* for *q*, `\t`, `\n`, `\r` for those controls; otherwise
   append *c* itself if it is printable (23.2), and otherwise its escape:
   `\x`*hh* if *c* < 0x100, `\u`*hhhh* if *c* < 0x10000, and `\U`*hhhhhhhh*
   otherwise, with lower-case hexadecimal digits.
3. Append *q* and return *r*.

ASCII(*o*) (used by `ascii()`, `!a`, and `%a`) is ToRepr(*o*) with every
non-ASCII code point of the result replaced by its escape as in step 2.

#### 23.1.4 Searching and testing

In this subclause, *start* and *end* are optional slice bounds (ToIndex or
`None`, interpreted as by SliceIndices, 25.4.2) restricting the part of *s*
examined; the substring argument must be a `str` ("*m*() argument 1 must be
str, not *tname*(*sub*)").

| Method | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `find(sub[, start[, end]])` | the lowest index at which *sub* occurs within the range, or −1 |
| `rfind(sub[, start[, end]])` | the highest such index, or −1 |
| `index`, `rindex` | as `find` and `rfind`, raising `ValueError` ("substring not found") instead of returning −1 |
| `count(sub[, start[, end]])` | the number of non-overlapping occurrences, scanning from the left; an empty *sub* occurs len + 1 times in the range |
| `startswith(prefix[, start[, end]])`, `endswith(suffix[, …])` | whether the range begins (ends) with the argument or with any element of a tuple of strings; another argument raises `TypeError` ("startswith first arg must be str or a tuple of str, not *tname*"), a tuple element that is not a str ("tuple for startswith must only contain str, not *tname*") |

An empty *sub* is found at *start* (for `find`) or at the end of the range (for
`rfind`), provided *start* ≤ len(*s*).

#### 23.1.5 Splitting and joining

`split(sep=None, maxsplit=-1)`:

1. If *sep* is `None`: discard leading white space (23.2); then repeatedly, while
   fewer than *maxsplit* splits have been made (or without limit if *maxsplit*
   is negative), take the maximal run of non-white-space characters as an item
   and discard the run of white space after it. When the limit is reached, the
   remainder, with leading white space discarded, is the last item if
   non-empty. No item is empty.
2. Otherwise *sep* shall be a non-empty `str` ("empty separator"). Cut *s* at
   each non-overlapping occurrence of *sep* from the left, at most *maxsplit*
   times; the items are the pieces between the cuts, empty ones included.

`rsplit` is the same working from the right, the items still being returned in
order. `splitlines(keepends=False)` cuts after each line boundary — `\n`, `\r`,
`\r\n`, `\v`, `\f`, `\x1c`, `\x1d`, `\x1e`, `\x85`, ` `, ` ` — keeping
the boundary in the items when *keepends* is true; a final boundary does not
start an empty item. `partition(sep)` returns (*before*, *sep*, *after*) for
the first occurrence of the non-empty *sep*, or (*s*, `''`, `''`);
`rpartition` uses the last occurrence and returns (`''`, `''`, *s*) when there
is none.

`sep.join(iterable)`: let *items* be the elements of ? GetIterator(*iterable*)
(a non-iterable raises `TypeError` "can only join an iterable"); each shall be a
`str` ("sequence item *i*: expected str instance, *tname* found"); return them
concatenated with *sep* between consecutive items.

#### 23.1.6 Transforming

| Method | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `replace(old, new, count=-1)` | *s* with the first *count* (all, when negative) non-overlapping occurrences of *old* replaced by *new*, scanning from the left; an empty *old* matches before each character and at the end |
| `removeprefix(p)`, `removesuffix(p)` | *s* without *p* at its start (end) if it is there; otherwise a copy of *s* |
| `strip(chars=None)`, `lstrip`, `rstrip` | *s* without the longest leading and/or trailing run of characters in *chars* (white space when `None`); *chars* shall be `None` or a `str` ("strip arg must be None or str") |
| `center(width, fillchar=' ')` | *s* padded with *fillchar* to *width*: with *p* = *width* − len(*s*) > 0, ⌊*p*/2⌋ + (1 if *p* and *width* are both odd) characters on the left and the rest on the right |
| `ljust`, `rjust` | padded on the right (left) |
| `zfill(width)` | padded on the left with `0` to *width*, after a leading `+` or `-` |
| `expandtabs(tabsize=8)` | each tab replaced by spaces to the next column that is a multiple of *tabsize* (none when *tabsize* ≤ 0), the column restarting after `\n` and `\r` |
| `upper()`, `lower()`, `casefold()`, `swapcase()` | the full Unicode case mappings (including the special casings and, for `lower`, the final sigma rule) and full case folding |
| `capitalize()` | the first character title-cased and the rest lower-cased |
| `title()` | each character title-cased if the preceding character is not cased, and lower-cased otherwise |
| `translate(table)` | each character *c* replaced by ? GetItem(*table*, ord(*c*)): an `int` code point, a `str`, or `None` to delete; a `LookupError` keeps *c*; any other result raises `TypeError` ("character mapping must return integer, None or str") |
| `encode(encoding='utf-8', errors='strict')` | ? Encode(*s*, *encoding*, *errors*) (23.3) |
| `format(*args, **kwargs)`, `format_map(mapping)` | 23.4 |

`fillchar` shall be a string of length one ("The fill character must be exactly
one character long"); widths and counts are converted by ToIndexSize.

The static method `str.maketrans(x[, y[, z]])` with one argument requires a
dict ("if you give only one argument to maketrans it must be a dict") whose
keys are code points or strings of length 1 ("string keys in translatetable
must be of length 1"), and returns a dict with each key converted to its code
point. With two strings of equal length ("the first two maketrans arguments
must have equal length") it maps each character of *x* to the one at the same
position of *y*; a third string maps each of its characters to `None`.

#### 23.1.7 Classification

The predicates return `False` for the empty string except where noted, and
otherwise whether every character satisfies the property (23.2):
`isalpha`, `isdecimal`, `isdigit`, `isnumeric`, `isalnum` (each character is
alphabetic or numeric), `isspace`, `isascii` (true also for the empty string),
and `isprintable` (true also for the empty string). `islower` and `isupper`
are true when the string has at least one cased character and no cased
character of the other case (or title case); `istitle` when it has at least one
cased character, every upper- or title-case character follows an uncased one,
and every lower-case character follows a cased one. `isidentifier` is true
when the string is an *identifier* of 12.3 (keywords included).

#### 23.1.8 Other members

`str.__format__(spec)` formats the string as 23.5 specifies. `str.__mod__` is
printf-style formatting (23.6). `str.__getnewargs__()` returns a tuple of a
copy of the string. `str.__len__` is the number of code points; `str.__iter__`
yields each character in order (a sequence iterator, 27.1.2).

### 23.2 Character properties

The properties of a code point are those of the Unicode Character Database
(clause 3):

| Property | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| alphabetic | general category Lu, Ll, Lt, Lm, or Lo |
| decimal | general category Nd |
| digit | Numeric_Type Decimal or Digit |
| numeric | Numeric_Type Decimal, Digit, or Numeric |
| white space | bidirectional class WS, B, or S, or general category Zs |
| printable | not of general category Cc, Cf, Cs, Co, Cn, Zl, Zp, or Zs, or U+0020 SPACE |
| cased, upper, lower, title | the derived properties Cased, Uppercase, Lowercase, and general category Lt |

The decimal value used by StringToInt and StringToFloat is the
Numeric_Value of a decimal character.

### 23.3 Encodings

#### 23.3.1 Codecs

A *codec* converts between strings and byte sequences. Every implementation
provides the codecs of Table 23-1; HostLookupCodec (Annex B) may provide others.
An encoding name is looked up after converting it to lower case and replacing
each space and `-` with `_`; a name that neither the table nor the host knows
raises a `LookupError` exception ("unknown encoding: *name*"). An encoding or
errors argument that is not a `str` raises `TypeError` ("*m*() argument
'encoding' must be str, not *tname*", and likewise for 'errors', *m* being the
method or constructor called).

Table 23-1: Core codecs

| Codec | Names | Encodes | Reason for an unencodable character | Reasons for undecodable bytes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `utf-8` | `utf_8`, `utf8`, `u8`, `cp65001` | RFC 3629 | "surrogates not allowed" | "invalid start byte", "invalid continuation byte", "unexpected end of data" |
| `ascii` | `ascii`, `us_ascii`, `646` | code points below 0x80 | "ordinal not in range(128)" | "ordinal not in range(128)" |
| `latin-1` | `latin_1`, `latin1`, `iso_8859_1`, `iso8859_1`, `l1`, `8859`, `cp819`, `latin` | code points below 0x100 | "ordinal not in range(256)" | (none) |

#### 23.3.2 Error handlers

Encode(*s*, *encoding*, *errors*) and Decode(*b*, *encoding*, *errors*) apply
the codec, and on each unencodable run of characters or undecodable run of
bytes construct the `UnicodeEncodeError` or `UnicodeDecodeError` (21.3.6)
describing it and consult the handler named by *errors*. The handler name is
looked up only when an error occurs; an unknown name raises `LookupError`
("unknown error handler name '*errors*'").

| Handler | On encoding | On decoding |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `strict` | raise the error | raise the error |
| `ignore` | skip | skip |
| `replace` | `?` | U+FFFD |
| `backslashreplace` | `\x`*hh*, `\u`*hhhh*, or `\U`*hhhhhhhh* | `\x`*hh* for each byte |
| `xmlcharrefreplace` | `&#`*decimal*`;` | raise `TypeError` ("don't know how to handle UnicodeDecodeError in error callback") |
| `surrogateescape` | a code point U+DC80–U+DCFF becomes the byte *c* − 0xDC00; others raise | each byte *b* ≥ 0x80 becomes U+DC00 + *b* |
| `surrogatepass` | (`utf-8`) surrogates are encoded as three bytes | (`utf-8`) three-byte surrogate sequences are decoded |

### 23.4 Format string syntax

`str.format(*args, **kwargs)` and `format_map(mapping)` (which uses *mapping*
for keyword lookup and no positional arguments, "Format string contains
positional fields") interpret the string by this grammar:

```text
format_string:     (literal_char | '{{' | '}}' | replacement_field)*
replacement_field: '{' [field_name] ['!' conversion] [':' format_spec] '}'
field_name:        arg_name ('.' attribute_name | '[' element_index ']')*
arg_name:          [identifier | digit+]
conversion:        'r' | 's' | 'a'
element_index:     digit+ | index_string
format_spec:       (literal_char | replacement_field)*
```

FormatString(*fmt*, *args*, *kwargs*, *depth*):

1. If *depth* < 0, raise a `ValueError` exception ("Max string recursion
   exceeded").
2. Scan *fmt*: `{{` and `}}` produce `{` and `}`; a single `}` raises
   `ValueError` ("Single '}' encountered in format string"); a `{` without its
   matching `}` raises `ValueError` ("Single '{' encountered in format string"
   at the end of the text, "expected '}' before end of string" inside a
   field). Within a field, brackets `[`…`]` are not scanned for braces, and
   braces nest.
3. For each replacement field, in order:
   1. Let *name* be the *arg_name*. If it is empty, use the next automatic
      index (0 at the start); if fields were already numbered manually, raise
      `ValueError` ("cannot switch from manual field specification to
      automatic field numbering"). If it is all digits, it is a manual index
      (the reverse switch raises "cannot switch from automatic field numbering
      to manual field specification"). A numeric name selects *args*[*n*]
      (`IndexError` "Replacement index *n* out of range for positional args
      tuple"); another name selects ? GetItem(*kwargs*, *name*).
   2. For each `.`*attr* apply ? GetAttr (an empty name raises `ValueError`
      "Empty attribute in format string"), and for each `[`*i*`]` apply
      ? GetItem with the integer *i* if it is all digits and the string *i*
      otherwise. After `]` only `.` or `[` may follow ("Only '.' or '[' may
      follow ']' in format field specifier").
   3. Apply the conversion (`!r` ToRepr, `!s` ToStr, `!a` ASCII); another
      letter raises `ValueError` ("Unknown conversion specifier *c*"), and a
      conversion of other than one letter before `:` or `}` raises
      `ValueError` ("expected ':' after conversion specifier").
   4. Let *spec* be ? FormatString(*format_spec*, *args*, *kwargs*, *depth* −
      1), sharing the automatic numbering state.
   5. Append ? Format(*value*, *spec*).
4. Return the concatenation. `str.format` calls FormatString with *depth* 2.

### 23.5 The format specification mini-language

#### 23.5.1 Syntax

```text
format_spec: [[fill] align] [sign] ['z'] ['#'] ['0'] [width] [grouping] ['.' precision [grouping]] [type]
fill:        <any character>
align:       '<' | '>' | '=' | '^'
sign:        '+' | '-' | ' '
width:       digit+
grouping:    ',' | '_'
precision:   digit+
type:        'b' | 'c' | 'd' | 'e' | 'E' | 'f' | 'F' | 'g' | 'G' | 'n' | 'o' | 's' | 'x' | 'X' | '%'
```

A specifier with characters left after *type* raises `ValueError` ("Invalid
format specifier '*spec*' for object of type '*tname*'"); a width or precision
too large for the index size raises `ValueError` ("Too many decimal digits in
format string"). Both `,` and `_` raise "Cannot specify both ',' and '_'.".

The `0` flag, when no *align* is given, selects `0` as the fill and `=` as the
alignment for numbers (`<` for strings). A type a value does not support raises
`ValueError` ("Unknown format code '*t*' for object of type '*tname*'").

#### 23.5.2 Padding and alignment

The formatted text is padded with *fill* (default space) to *width* code
points: `<` pads on the right, `>` on the left, `^` puts ⌊*p*/2⌋ on the left
and the rest on the right, and `=` pads between the sign (and base prefix) and
the digits. Numbers default to `>`; strings to `<`. When padding with `=` and
grouping, the padding zeros themselves are grouped, so that `format(1234,
'010,')` is `'00,001,234'`.

#### 23.5.3 Strings

`str.__format__(spec)` accepts type `s` or none. *precision* truncates the
string to that many code points. A sign raises `ValueError` ("Sign not allowed
in string format specifier"), `=` ("'=' alignment not allowed in string format
specifier"), `#` ("Alternate form (#) not allowed in string format
specifier"), `z` ("Negative zero coercion (z) not allowed in string format
specifier"), and a grouping ("Cannot specify ',' with 's'.").

#### 23.5.4 Integers

`int.__format__(spec)` accepts the types `b`, `o`, `x`, `X` (bases 2, 8, 16, 16
with upper-case digits), `d` or none (base 10), `n` (base 10 with the host's
locale grouping, none in the Core profile), `c` (the character with that code
point, `OverflowError` "%c arg not in range(0x110000)"), and the float types,
for which the value is first converted by IntToFloat. For an integer type:

- *precision* raises `ValueError` ("Precision not allowed in integer format
  specifier") and `z` ("Negative zero coercion (z) not allowed in integer
  format specifier");
- the sign is `-` for negatives only (default), `+` for all, or a space for
  non-negatives;
- `#` adds the prefix `0b`, `0o`, `0x`, or `0X`;
- `,` groups decimal digits by three and is allowed only with `d` or none;
  `_` groups decimal digits by three and binary, octal, and hexadecimal digits
  by four; otherwise "Cannot specify '*g*' with '*t*'.";
- `c` refuses a sign ("Sign not allowed with integer format specifier 'c'")
  and `#` ("Alternate form (#) not allowed with integer format specifier
  'c'").

The integer string conversion limit (22.1.8) applies to base 10.

#### 23.5.5 Floating-point numbers

`float.__format__(spec)` accepts the types below. Rounding is to the nearest
representable decimal, ties to even, applied to the exact binary value.
*P* is *precision*, defaulting to 6.

| Type | Presentation |
| --- | --- |
| `e`, `E` | one digit, a point (omitted when *P* is 0 unless `#`), *P* digits, `e` or `E`, the exponent's sign, and at least two exponent digits |
| `f`, `F` | fixed point with *P* digits after the point (point omitted as for `e`) |
| `g`, `G` | with *p* = max(*P*, 1) and *x* rounded to *p* significant digits having decimal exponent *E*: `e` with precision *p* − 1 if *E* < −4 or *E* ≥ *p*, else `f` with precision *p* − 1 − *E*; trailing zeros and a trailing point are removed unless `#` |
| `n` | `g` with the host's locale (Core: as `g`) |
| `%` | the value × 100 as `f`, followed by `%` |
| none | without *precision*, FloatRepr (22.3.4); with it, as `g` but switching to exponent form when *E* ≥ *p* − 1, and keeping at least one digit after the point |

Infinities and NaNs are written `inf` and `nan` (`INF`, `NAN` for `E`, `F`,
`G`), with their sign. The `z` option turns a negative zero result, after
rounding, into a positive one. `,` and `_` group the integer digits by three; a
*grouping* after the precision groups the fractional digits by three. Types
`b`, `c`, `d`, `o`, `x`, `X` raise the error for an unknown format code.

#### 23.5.6 Complex numbers

`complex.__format__(spec)` formats the real and imaginary parts as `float`
values with the same options (the sign applying to the real part, the
imaginary part always signed), joins them as *re* *im*`j`, and pads the result.
With type none the result is as ComplexRepr (22.4.3) when there is no other
option, and otherwise the parts follow the type-none float rules, the real
part omitted when it is +0, and the result parenthesized. `=` alignment and
the `0` flag raise `ValueError` ("'=' alignment flag is not allowed in complex
format specifier", "Zero padding is not allowed in complex format
specifier").

#### 23.5.7 Other objects

`object.__format__(spec)` returns ToStr(*self*) when *spec* is empty and
otherwise raises `TypeError` ("unsupported format string passed to
*tname*(*self*).\_\_format\_\_"). `bool` formats as `int` with a non-empty
spec. Types whose `__format__` is not otherwise specified inherit
`object.__format__`.

### 23.6 printf-style formatting

`s % values` (`str.__mod__`) produces a string from the template *s*. If
*values* is a tuple, its elements are the arguments; if it is a mapping (and not
a tuple or str) and *s* uses mapping keys, it supplies them; otherwise *values*
is the single argument. Each `%` starts a conversion:

```text
conversion: '%' ['(' mapping_key ')'] flag* [width | '*'] ['.' (precision | '*')] [length_modifier] type
flag:       '#' | '0' | '-' | ' ' | '+'
length_modifier: 'h' | 'l' | 'L'
type:       'd' | 'i' | 'u' | 'o' | 'x' | 'X' | 'e' | 'E' | 'f' | 'F' | 'g' | 'G' | 'c' | 'r' | 's' | 'a' | '%'
```

1. `%%` produces `%`. A `%` at the end raises `ValueError` ("stray % at
   position *i*"), an unknown type ("unsupported format %*c* at position
   *i*").
2. A mapping key selects ? GetItem(*values*, *key*); it requires a mapping
   ("format requires a mapping, not *tname*"), and once used every conversion
   needs one ("format requires a parenthesised mapping key at position *i*").
3. `*` takes the width or precision from the next argument, which shall be an
   `int` ("format argument *n*: * requires int, not *tname*").
4. Too few arguments raise `TypeError` ("not enough arguments for format
   string (got *n*)"); arguments left over when *values* is a tuple (or a
   single non-mapping argument not consumed) raise `TypeError` ("not all
   arguments converted during string formatting (required *k*, got *n*)").
5. `d`, `i`, `u` require a real number ("format argument: %*t* requires a real
   number, not *tname*"), converted by `int`; `o`, `x`, `X` an integer
   ("… requires an integer, not *tname*", via ToIndex). The precision is a
   minimum number of digits; `#` adds `0o`, `0x`, or `0X`.
6. `e`, `E`, `f`, `F`, `g`, `G` convert by `float` and format as 23.5.5 with
   precision default 6 (`#` keeps the point and, for `g`, trailing zeros).
7. `c` takes an integer in range(0x110000) (`OverflowError` "format argument:
   %c argument not in range(0x110000)") or a string of length 1 (`TypeError`
   "format argument: %c requires an integer or a unicode character, not a
   string of length *n*").
8. `s`, `r`, `a` convert by ToStr, ToRepr, ASCII; a precision truncates.
9. The width pads on the left with spaces, on the right with `-`, and with
   zeros after the sign and prefix with `0` (numbers only; `-` overrides `0`).
   `+` signs non-negative numbers and a space prefixes them with a space.

### 23.7 Templates and interpolations

%Template% and %Interpolation% are intrinsic types whose `__module__` is
`"string.templatelib"`; neither is an acceptable base type. A t-string (13.2.5)
creates them. They are reachable from a program as `type(t'')` and
`type(t'{x}'.interpolations[0])`; Annex C describes the library module that
binds them by name.

#### 23.7.1 Template ( *args* )

Accepts only positional arguments ("Template.\_\_new\_\_ only accepts \*args
arguments"), each a `str` or an %Interpolation% ("Template.\_\_new\_\_ \*args
need to be of type 'str' or 'Interpolation', got *tname*"). Consecutive
strings are concatenated, and an empty string is supplied between consecutive
interpolations and at either end, so that `strings` has exactly one more
element than `interpolations`.

| Member | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `strings` | tuple of the literal parts |
| `interpolations` | tuple of the interpolations |
| `values` | tuple of the interpolations' values |
| `__iter__()` | iterates over the parts in order, skipping empty strings |
| `__add__(other)` | for a %Template% *other*, the template of the parts of both (the last string of the first joined to the first of the second); otherwise `TypeError` ("can only concatenate string.templatelib.Template (not "*tname*") to string.templatelib.Template") |
| `__repr__()` | "Template(strings=*r1*, interpolations=*r2*)" with the reprs of the two tuples |

`str + Template` raises the `TypeError` of 23.1.2. Templates compare by
identity.

#### 23.7.2 Interpolation ( *value*, *expression*=`''`, *conversion*=None, *format_spec*=`''` )

*expression* and *format_spec* shall be `str` ("Interpolation() argument
'*name*' must be str, not *tname*"); *conversion* shall be `None`, `'s'`,
`'r'`, or `'a'` (`ValueError` "Interpolation() argument 'conversion' must be
one of 's', 'a' or 'r'"). The four arguments are read-only attributes
("readonly attribute"). `__match_args__` is (`'value'`, `'expression'`,
`'conversion'`, `'format_spec'`). `repr` is "Interpolation(*r*, *r*, *r*,
*r*)" with the reprs of the four attributes.

## 24 Binary Data

### 24.1 The `bytes` type

A `bytes` value is an immutable finite sequence of integers in range(0, 256).
Indexing yields an `int`; slicing yields a `bytes`. Equal byte sequences have
equal hashes. A `bytes` never compares equal to a `str` (a host may issue a
`BytesWarning` for the comparison, Annex B), and ordering comparisons with a
`str` raise the `TypeError` of RichCompare.

#### 24.1.1 bytes ( [ *source* [ , *encoding* [ , *errors* ] ] ] )

1. If no argument is given, return an empty `bytes`.
2. If *source* is a `str`: if *encoding* is not given, raise a `TypeError`
   exception ("string argument without an encoding"); otherwise return
   ? Encode(*source*, *encoding*, *errors*) (23.3).
3. If *encoding* or *errors* is given, raise a `TypeError` exception
   ("encoding without a string argument" or, when only *errors* is given,
   "errors without a string argument").
4. Let *m* be ? LookupSpecial(*source*, `"__bytes__"`). If it is not **absent**,
   let *r* be ? Call(*m*, «»); if *r* is not a `bytes` (or subclass), raise a
   `TypeError` exception ("*fqname*(*source*).`__bytes__`() must return a
   bytes, not *tname*(*r*)"); return a `bytes` with the contents of *r*.
5. If *source* supports the buffer protocol (24.4), return a copy of its bytes.
6. If LookupSpecial(*source*, `"__index__"`) is not **absent**, let *n* be
   ? ToIndexSize(*source*); if *n* < 0 raise a `ValueError` exception
   ("negative count"); return *n* zero bytes.
7. If *source* is iterable, return the bytes of its elements, each converted by
   ToIndex ("'*tname*' object cannot be interpreted as an integer") and in
   range(0, 256) (`ValueError` "bytes must be in range(0, 256)").
8. Raise a `TypeError` exception ("cannot convert '*tname*(*source*)' object to
   bytes").

When the class called is a strict subclass of `bytes`, the result is an
instance of it. `bytes.__new__(T)` for a non-subtype *T* raises `TypeError`
("bytes.\_\_new\_\_(*T*): *T* is not a subtype of bytes").

#### 24.1.2 BytesRepr ( *b* )

The repr of a `bytes` is `b` followed by its contents quoted as StrRepr (23.1.3)
quotes a string, except that the quote is chosen the same way, each byte below
0x20 or at least 0x7F other than `\t`, `\n`, `\r` is written `\x`*hh*, and the
other bytes are written as their ASCII characters. `str(b)` is `repr(b)`.

#### 24.1.3 fromhex and hex

The class method `fromhex(string)` takes a `str` or bytes-like object
("fromhex() argument must be str or bytes-like, not *tname*") of pairs of
hexadecimal digits, ignoring ASCII white space between pairs, and returns an
instance of the class it is called on. A character other than a digit or white
space raises `ValueError` ("non-hexadecimal number found in fromhex() arg at
position *i*"), and an odd digit at the end ("fromhex() arg must contain an
even number of hexadecimal digits").

`hex(sep, bytes_per_sep=1)` returns the bytes as lower-case hexadecimal pairs;
when *sep* (a `str` or `bytes` of length 1, "sep must be length 1.") is given,
it is inserted between groups of *bytes_per_sep* bytes, counted from the right
(from the left when *bytes_per_sep* is negative).

### 24.2 The `bytearray` type

A `bytearray` is a mutable sequence of integers in range(0, 256). It is
constructed as `bytes` is (24.1.1, without `__bytes__` taking part for a
`bytearray` subclass result), its repr is "bytearray(*r*)" with *r* the
BytesRepr of its contents, and it is unhashable. Indexing yields an `int`;
slicing yields a `bytearray`.

Beyond the operations of 24.3 and the mutable sequence operations (25.5.4):

- Item assignment takes an integer in range(0, 256) (`ValueError` "byte must be
  in range(0, 256)"; a non-integer raises the error of ToIndex); slice
  assignment takes a bytes-like object or an iterable of such integers
  ("can assign only bytes, buffers, or iterables of ints in range(0, 256)") and
  may change the length.
- `append`, `extend`, `insert`, `pop` ("pop from empty bytearray"), `remove`
  ("value not found in bytearray"), `clear`, `copy`, and `reverse` behave as
  the list methods (25.1) for byte values; `+=` and `*=` mutate in place.
- An operation that would change the length while a buffer export of the
  `bytearray` is not released raises a `BufferError` exception ("Existing
  exports of data: object cannot be re-sized"); writes that keep the length
  are allowed.

### 24.3 Bytes and bytearray operations

`bytes` and `bytearray` provide the methods of 23.1.4–23.1.7 with these
differences:

1. Arguments that are byte sequences accept any bytes-like object; a `str`
   raises `TypeError` ("a bytes-like object is required, not 'str'"; for
   `startswith` and `endswith` "startswith first arg must be bytes or a tuple
   of bytes, not *tname*"). The searching methods and `in` also accept an
   integer in range(0, 256) for a single byte ("argument should be integer or
   bytes-like object, not '*tname*'", `ValueError` "byte must be in range(0,
   256)").
2. Results are of the receiver's type; methods that return the receiver's
   contents unchanged still return a new object for a `bytearray`.
3. Case mappings, `title`, `capitalize`, `swapcase`, the predicates, and the
   white space of `split` and `strip` consider only ASCII: letters are
   `a`–`z` and `A`–`Z`, digits `0`–`9`, and white space the bytes
   `\t\n\v\f\r` and space. There is no title case and no `casefold`,
   `isdecimal`, `isnumeric`, `isidentifier`, `isprintable`, or `format`.
   `splitlines` recognizes only `\n`, `\r`, and `\r\n`.
4. The fill argument of `center`, `ljust`, and `rjust` is a byte string of
   length 1 ("center() argument 2 must be a byte string of length 1, not
   *tname*").
5. `join` requires bytes-like items ("sequence item *i*: expected a
   bytes-like object, *tname* found").
6. `translate(table, /, delete=b'')` deletes the bytes in *delete* and maps the
   rest through *table*, a bytes-like object of exactly 256 bytes
   ("translation table must be 256 characters long") or `None`.
   `bytes.maketrans(from, to)` returns the 256-byte table mapping each byte of
   *from* to that of *to* ("maketrans arguments must have same length").
7. `decode(encoding='utf-8', errors='strict')` returns ? Decode (23.3).
8. `b % values` is printf-style formatting (23.6) producing bytes: `s` and `b`
   take a bytes-like object or an object with `__bytes__` ("format argument:
   %b requires a bytes-like object, or an object that implements \_\_bytes\_\_,
   not *tname*"), `r` and `a` are ASCII(*v*) encoded as ASCII, and `c` takes
   an integer in range(256) (`OverflowError` "format argument: %c argument not
   in range(256)") or a bytes object of length 1.
9. Concatenation with a `str` raises `TypeError` ("can't concat str to
   *tname*"); the result type is that of the left operand.

### 24.4 The buffer protocol and `memoryview`

#### 24.4.1 Buffers

An object *supports the buffer protocol*, or is *bytes-like*, if its type is
`bytes`, `bytearray`, `memoryview`, or a subclass of them, or if its type
defines `__buffer__`. GetBuffer(*o*, *flags*):

1. If *o* is a `bytes`, `bytearray`, or `memoryview`, return a new export of
   its memory (for a `bytearray`, the export count increases until it is
   released).
2. Let *m* be ? LookupSpecial(*o*, `"__buffer__"`). If **absent**, raise a
   `TypeError` exception ("a bytes-like object is required, not
   '*tname*(*o*)'", or the caller's message).
3. Let *v* be ? Call(*m*, « *flags* »). If *v* is not a `memoryview`, raise a
   `TypeError` exception ("*fqname*(*o*).`__buffer__`() must return a
   memoryview, not *tname*(*v*)").
4. Return *v*, remembering *o* as its exporter.

ReleaseBuffer(*v*): if the exporter's type defines `__release_buffer__`, call
it with *v*; then release *v*. A consumer that obtained a buffer only to copy
it (such as `bytes(o)`) releases it before returning.

#### 24.4.2 memoryview ( *object* )

Returns a view of ? GetBuffer(*object*) (`TypeError` "memoryview: a bytes-like
object is required, not '*tname*'"). A view has a format, an item size, a
shape, and strides; the Core profile provides one-dimensional views and
multi-dimensional views created by `cast`, with the formats `B`, `b`, `c`,
`h`, `H`, `i`, `I`, `l`, `L`, `q`, `Q`, `n`, `N`, `e`, `f`, `d`, `?`, and `P` in
native size and byte order (Annex D).

| Member | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `len(m)`, `m[i]`, `m[i:j:k]` | the number of items, an item converted by the format (`IndexError` "index out of bounds on dimension 1"; non-integer keys `TypeError` "memoryview: invalid slice key"), a view of the selected items |
| `m[i] = v`, `m[i:j] = b` | write an item ("cannot modify read-only memory" for a read-only view; `TypeError` "memoryview: invalid type for format '*f*'"; `ValueError` "memoryview: invalid value for format '*f*'") or a same-shaped buffer ("memoryview assignment: lvalue and rvalue have different structures") |
| `obj`, `nbytes`, `readonly`, `itemsize`, `format`, `ndim`, `shape`, `strides`, `suboffsets`, `c_contiguous`, `f_contiguous`, `contiguous` | the view's properties |
| `tobytes(order='C')`, `tolist()`, `hex(…)` | the contents as `bytes`, as a (nested) list of items, as `bytes.hex` |
| `toreadonly()` | a read-only view of the same memory |
| `cast(format[, shape])` | a view with another format and shape ("memoryview: length is not a multiple of itemsize") |
| `release()`, `__enter__()`, `__exit__(…)` | release the view (idempotent); `with m:` releases it on exit |
| `==` | equal when the shapes and the items, compared as values, are equal; also with any bytes-like object |
| `hash(m)` | for a read-only view, the hash of `m.tobytes()`; otherwise `ValueError` ("cannot hash writable memoryview object") |

Any operation other than `release` and `repr` on a released view raises
`ValueError` ("operation forbidden on released memoryview object"). Ordering
comparisons are not defined. `repr(m)` is "\<memory at *address*\>", or
"\<released memory at *address*\>".

## 25 Sequences

A *sequence* is an object that supports Length and integer subscription from 0
to its length − 1. The built-in sequences are `str`, `bytes`, `bytearray`,
`memoryview`, `list`, `tuple`, and `range`; `bytearray` and `list` are
*mutable sequences*. This clause specifies the `list`, `tuple`, `range`, and `slice` types and, in
25.5, the common sequence operations, which apply to every sequence unless its
type's clause says otherwise.

### 25.1 The `list` type

#### 25.1.1 list ( [ *iterable* ] )

Returns a new list of the items of ? GetIterator(*iterable*), or an empty list.
`list` takes no keyword arguments ("list() takes no keyword arguments") and at
most one argument ("list expected at most 1 argument, got *n*"). The arguments
are consumed by `list.__init__`, which clears the list first, so that
`__init__` called again on a list replaces its contents. `list.__new__(T)` for
a non-subtype *T* raises `TypeError` ("list.\_\_new\_\_(*T*): *T* is not a
subtype of list").

#### 25.1.2 Representation

`repr(l)` is `[`, the reprs of the items separated by `", "`, and `]`. A list
already being represented by the same execution context is written `[...]`.

#### 25.1.3 Methods

| Method | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| `append(x)` | add *x* at the end |
| `extend(iterable)` | append the items of the iterable (of a copy, when it is the list itself) |
| `insert(i, x)` | insert before index *i*, clamped to [0, *n*] after adding *n* to a negative *i* |
| `pop(i=-1)` | remove and return the item at *i* ("pop from empty list"; "pop index out of range") |
| `remove(x)` | remove the first item that is *x* or equals *x* ("list.remove(x): x not in list") |
| `clear()`, `copy()`, `reverse()` | empty the list; return a shallow copy; reverse in place |
| `index`, `count` | 25.5.2 |
| `sort(*, key=None, reverse=False)` | 25.1.4 |

#### 25.1.4 sort ( *, *key*=None, *reverse*=False )

1. `sort` takes keyword arguments only ("sort() takes no positional
   arguments").
2. Let *items* be the items of the list, and empty the list for the duration of
   the sort.
3. If *key* is not `None`, let *keys* be ? Call(*key*, «*x*») for each item
   *x*, in order; otherwise let *keys* be *items*. If a call raises, restore
   *items* and propagate.
4. Sort *items* by *keys* stably, comparing two keys *a* and *b* only by
   ToBoolean(? RichCompare(*a*, *b*, `<`)). If *reverse* is true, the result
   is as if the list were reversed, sorted, and reversed again, so that
   stability is preserved. If a comparison raises, the list holds all of
   *items* in an implementation-defined order and the exception propagates.
5. If the list was modified during the sort, raise a `ValueError` exception
   ("list modified during sort") after restoring the sorted *items*.
6. Store *items* in the list and return `None`.

The algorithm, and therefore the number and order of comparisons, is
implementation-defined (Annex D); a conforming sort makes O(*n* log *n*)
comparisons.

#### 25.1.5 List iteration

A list iterator holds the list and an index, initially 0. Each `__next__`
returns the item at the index and increments it, or, if the index is not less
than the list's current length, releases the list and raises `StopIteration`;
items added during iteration are therefore visited. `reversed(l)` returns an
iterator starting at the last index and stepping down, ending if the index
falls outside the current length. Both have `__length_hint__`, and support
`__reduce__` and `__setstate__` (Annex C).

### 25.2 The `tuple` type

`tuple(iterable=(), /)` returns a tuple of the items of the iterable; at most
one argument ("tuple expected at most 1 argument, got *n*"). Whether
`tuple(t)` for an exact tuple *t* returns *t* itself, and whether every empty
tuple is the same object, is implementation-defined (Annex D).

`repr(t)` is `(` and the reprs of the items separated by `", "`, with a
trailing `,` for a one-item tuple, and `)`. A recursive tuple (possible
through a mutable item) is written `(...)`.

A tuple is hashable when its items are. TupleHash(*t*) uses unsigned 64-bit
wrapping arithmetic with *P1* = 11400714785074694791, *P2* =
14029467366897019727, and *P5* = 2870177450012600261:

1. Let *acc* be *P5*.
2. For each item *x*: let *lane* be ? Hash(*x*) as an unsigned 64-bit value;
   set *acc* to *acc* + *lane* × *P2*; rotate *acc* left by 31 bits; set *acc*
   to *acc* × *P1*.
3. Set *acc* to *acc* + (len(*t*) XOR (*P5* XOR 3527539)).
4. If *acc*, as a signed value, is −1, return 1546275796; otherwise return
   *acc* as a signed value.

(Annex D permits a 32-bit hash width with the corresponding constants.)

### 25.3 The `range` type

A `range` represents an immutable arithmetic progression of integers.

#### 25.3.1 range ( *stop* ) and range ( *start*, *stop*, *step*=1 )

1. One to three arguments are accepted ("range expected at least 1 argument,
   got 0"; "range expected at most 3 arguments, got *n*"), and no keywords.
2. Each argument is converted by ToIndex. With one argument *start* is 0.
3. If *step* is 0, raise a `ValueError` exception ("range() arg 3 must not be
   zero").
4. Return a range with those `start`, `stop`, and `step` attributes
   (read-only, "readonly attribute").

The *length* of a range is the number of integers *start* + *k* × *step*, *k*
≥ 0, that lie before *stop* in the direction of *step*. `len(r)` raises an
`OverflowError` exception ("Python int too large to convert to C ssize_t")
when the length is outside the index-size range; subscription and the other
operations work with the exact length.

#### 25.3.2 Operations

- `r[i]` is *start* + *i* × *step* for 0 ≤ *i* < length, negative *i* counting
  from the end ("range object index out of range").
- `r[i:j:k]` is the range whose items are the selected items: its start is the
  item at the slice's first index, its step *step* × *k*, and its stop
  computed so that the length matches.
- `x in r`, `r.count(x)`, and `r.index(x)` are computed arithmetically when *x*
  is an `int` (or `bool`), and by scanning with `==` otherwise; `index` raises
  `ValueError` ("range.index(x): x not in range" for an integer,
  "sequence.index(x): x not in sequence" otherwise).
- Two ranges are equal when they represent the same sequence: equal lengths,
  and, unless the length is 0, the same start, and, unless the length is 1, the
  same step. The hash is TupleHash of (length, `None`, `None`) when the length
  is 0, (1, *start*, `None`) when it is 1, and (length, *start*, *step*)
  otherwise. Ordering comparisons, concatenation, and repetition are not
  defined.
- `repr(r)` is "range(*start*, *stop*)" when *step* is 1 and "range(*start*,
  *stop*, *step*)" otherwise. `reversed(r)` iterates from the last item down.

### 25.4 The `slice` type

#### 25.4.1 slice ( *stop* ) and slice ( *start*, *stop*, *step*=None )

Returns a slice object with read-only attributes `start`, `stop`, and `step`
(`None` when omitted, of any type). One to three arguments ("slice expected at
least 1 argument, got 0"; "slice expected at most 3 arguments, got *n*").
Slices compare as the tuples (*start*, *stop*, *step*) and are hashable when
their parts are. `repr` is "slice(*r*, *r*, *r*)". `slice[T]` is a generic
alias (29.9).

#### 25.4.2 SliceIndices ( *slice*, *length* )

1. If *step* is `None`, let *k* be 1; otherwise let *k* be its ToIndex value
   (a non-integer raises `TypeError` "slice indices must be integers or None or
   have an \_\_index\_\_ method"). If *k* is 0, raise a `ValueError` exception
   ("slice step cannot be zero").
2. Let *lo* be −1 and *hi* be *length* − 1 if *k* < 0, and *lo* be 0 and *hi*
   be *length* otherwise.
3. If *start* is `None`, let *a* be *hi* if *k* < 0 and *lo* otherwise.
   Otherwise let *a* be its ToIndex value; if *a* < 0, set *a* to *a* +
   *length* and, if still less than *lo*, to *lo*; if *a* > *hi*, set *a* to
   *hi*.
4. Compute *b* from *stop* likewise, with `None` meaning *lo* if *k* < 0 and
   *hi* otherwise.
5. Return (*a*, *b*, *k*). The selected indices are *a*, *a* + *k*, …, while
   less than *b* (for *k* > 0) or greater than *b* (for *k* < 0).

`slice.indices(length)` returns SliceIndices as a tuple; *length* is
converted by ToIndex, and a negative one raises `ValueError` ("length should
not be negative"). For subscription the bounds are converted with the message
"slice indices must be integers or have an \_\_index\_\_ method", and values
outside the index-size range are clamped to it.

### 25.5 Common sequence operations

#### 25.5.1 NormalizeIndex ( *s*, *i* )

1. Let *n* be ? ToIndex(*i*); a non-integer raises a `TypeError` exception
   ("*tname*(*s*) indices must be integers or slices, not *tname*(*i*)").
2. If *n* < 0, set *n* to *n* + Length(*s*).
3. If *n* < 0 or *n* ≥ Length(*s*), raise an `IndexError` exception ("*tname*(*s*)
   index out of range"; for an assignment "*tname*(*s*) assignment index out
   of range"). An *n* outside the index-size range raises an `IndexError`
   exception ("cannot fit 'int' into an index-sized integer").
4. Return *n*.

#### 25.5.2 Common operations

For a sequence *s* of length *n*:

| Operation | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `s[i]` | the item at NormalizeIndex(*s*, *i*) |
| `s[i:j:k]` | a new sequence of the same type (a `list` for a `list` subclass, and so on) holding the items at the indices that SliceIndices (25.4.2) selects |
| `x in s` | **true** if some item *e* has *e* is *x* or ? RichCompare(*e*, *x*, `==`) is true, scanning from the start |
| `s + t` | a new sequence of the items of *s* then *t*; *t* shall be of the same kind ("can only concatenate *tname*(*s*) (not "*tname*(*t*)") to *tname*(*s*)") |
| `s * m`, `m * s` | *s* repeated ToIndexSize(*m*) times, empty for *m* ≤ 0; a non-integer *m* declines, making BinaryOp raise "can't multiply sequence by non-int of type '*tname*(*m*)'" |
| `s.count(x)` | the number of items *e* with *e* is *x* or *e* == *x* |
| `s.index(x[, i[, j]])` | the least index in the slice range [*i*:*j*] whose item is *x* or equals *x*; otherwise `ValueError` ("*tname*.index(x): x not in *tname*"); *i* and *j* shall be integers ("slice indices must be integers or have an \_\_index\_\_ method") |
| `len(s)`, `iter(s)`, `reversed(s)` | *n*; an iterator over the items in order; in reverse |

The sequence results are of the base built-in type, not of a subclass.
`range`, `memoryview`, and `bytearray` repetition and concatenation are as
their clauses specify (`range` has neither).

#### 25.5.3 SequenceCompare ( *s*, *t*, *op* )

Sequences of the same kind (`list` with `list`, `tuple` with `tuple`) compare
lexicographically:

1. Let *i* be the least index less than both lengths such that *s*[*i*] is not
   *t*[*i*] and ? RichCompare(*s*[*i*], *t*[*i*], `==`) is false, if any.
2. If there is no such *i*: compare the lengths with *op*.
3. Otherwise: if *op* is `==` return **false**; if `!=` return **true**; else
   return ? RichCompare(*s*[*i*], *t*[*i*], *op*).

Sequences of different kinds compare unequal and are unordered.

#### 25.5.4 Mutable sequence operations

For a mutable sequence *s*:

| Operation | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| `s[i] = x`, `del s[i]` | replace or remove the item at NormalizeIndex (assignment message) |
| `s[i:j] = t` | replace the selected items, step 1, by the items of the iterable *t* ("must assign iterable to extended slice"); *t* may be *s* itself (a copy is taken first) |
| `s[i:j:k] = t` | for a step other than 1, *t* shall have exactly as many items as are selected ("attempt to assign sequence of size *m* to extended slice of size *n*") |
| `del s[i:j:k]` | remove the selected items |
| `s += t`, `s *= m` | extend by the items of *t*; repeat in place (clear for *m* ≤ 0); both return *s* |

## 26 Mappings and Sets

`dict`, `frozendict`, `set`, and `frozenset` store their keys (elements) by
hash. Two keys *a* and *b* are *the same key* when Hash(*a*) = Hash(*b*) and
either *a* is *b* or ? RichCompare(*a*, *b*, `==`) is true; equal numbers of
different types (`1`, `1.0`, `True`) are therefore the same key. Every lookup,
insertion, and deletion computes ? Hash(*key*) first; when that raises the
`TypeError` of Hash for an unhashable key, the exception is replaced by a
`TypeError` exception ("cannot use '*tname*(*key*)' as a dict key (unhashable
type: '*tname*(*key*)')", or "as a set element" for sets). Other exceptions
raised by `__hash__` or `__eq__` propagate.

### 26.1 The `dict` type

A `dict` is a mutable mapping that remembers the order in which keys were
first inserted. Replacing the value of a present key keeps the key object
already stored and its position; deleting a key and inserting it again puts it
at the end.

#### 26.1.1 dict ( [ *source* ], **kwargs )

1. At most one positional argument is accepted ("dict expected at most 1
   argument, got *n*").
2. Let *d* be a new empty dict (an instance of the class called).
3. If *source* is given, perform ? DictUpdate(*d*, *source*).
4. For each keyword argument, in order, perform ? DictSetItem(*d*, *name*,
   *value*).
5. Return *d*.

DictUpdate(*d*, *source*):

1. If *source* is a `dict` or `frozendict` whose `keys` method is the
   built-in one, or has an attribute `keys`, perform DictMerge(*d*, *source*,
   **false**) (13.2.3).
2. Otherwise, for each element *e* of ? GetIterator(*source*), at index *i*:
   let *pair* be ? IterateToList(*e*) (a non-iterable *e* raises the
   `TypeError` of GetIterator); if its length is not 2, raise a `ValueError`
   exception ("dictionary update sequence element #*i* has length *m*; 2 is
   required"); perform ? DictSetItem(*d*, *pair*[0], *pair*[1]).

`dict.fromkeys(iterable, value=None)` (a class method) creates an instance of
the class and sets each element of the iterable to *value*.

#### 26.1.2 Operations

| Operation | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `d[k]` | the value of the same key; if absent and Type(*d*) is a subclass of `dict` defining `__missing__`, ? Call of `__missing__` with *k*; otherwise `KeyError` with argument *k* |
| `d[k] = v`, `del d[k]` | DictSetItem; remove the key (`KeyError` *k* when absent) |
| `k in d`, `len(d)`, `iter(d)` | membership; the number of keys; an iterator over the keys in order |
| `reversed(d)` | an iterator over the keys in reverse order |
| `d == e` | for two mappings of these types: the same keys, each with equal values (compared by identity or `==`); order is ignored |
| `d \| e`, `d \|= e` | a copy of *d* updated from the `dict` (or `frozendict`) *e*; `\|=` updates *d* in place by DictUpdate, so it also accepts pairs |
| `repr(d)` | `{`, each key's repr, `": "`, and the value's repr, separated by `", "`, and `}`; a dict already being represented is written `{...}` |

Ordering comparisons are not defined, and a `dict` is unhashable.

#### 26.1.3 Methods

| Method | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `get(k, default=None)` | the value, or *default* (`__missing__` is not consulted) |
| `setdefault(k, default=None)` | the value, after inserting *default* if absent |
| `pop(k[, default])` | remove and return the value; if absent, *default* or `KeyError` |
| `popitem()` | remove and return the last inserted (key, value) pair (`KeyError` "popitem(): dictionary is empty") |
| `update([source], **kwargs)` | DictUpdate then the keywords ("update expected at most 1 argument, got *n*") |
| `keys()`, `values()`, `items()` | views (26.4) |
| `copy()`, `clear()` | a shallow copy of type `dict`; remove all keys |

#### 26.1.4 Mutation during iteration

An iterator over a dict or one of its views records the dict's size and a
version. If the dict's size differs from the recorded one when `__next__` is
called, the iterator raises a `RuntimeError` exception ("dictionary changed
size during iteration") and is exhausted afterwards. If keys were removed and
others added so that the size is unchanged, a later call of `__next__` raises a
`RuntimeError` exception ("dictionary keys changed during iteration") and the
iterator is exhausted afterwards; the call that would produce more keys than the
recorded size raises it at the latest, and which keys are produced before it is
implementation-defined (Annex D). Replacing values does not affect iteration.

### 26.2 The `frozendict` type

A `frozendict` is an immutable mapping with the ordering, construction
(`frozendict(…)` as 26.1.1), reading operations, `fromkeys`, `copy` (which may
return the object itself), views, and equality of `dict`. It is not a subclass
of `dict`. It has no mutating methods; item assignment and deletion raise
`TypeError` ("'frozendict' object does not support item assignment" / "…item
deletion"). `x | y` returns an object of the type of the left operand, and
`x |= y` for a `frozendict` *x* binds a new `frozendict`. A frozendict is
hashable when its values are: its hash is that of a `frozenset` of its items
(26.3.4). `repr` is "frozendict(*r*)" with *r* the dict repr of its items, or
"frozendict()" when empty.

### 26.3 The `set` and `frozenset` types

A `set` is a mutable, unordered collection of distinct hashable elements; a
`frozenset` is its immutable, hashable counterpart. The iteration order of
a set is implementation-defined but is the same for repeated iterations of an
unmodified set.

#### 26.3.1 set ( [ *iterable* ] ) and frozenset ( [ *iterable* ] )

Return a new set of the elements of the iterable, added in order by SetAdd.
SetAdd(*s*, *x*) adds *x* unless the same element (see the introduction to this clause) is present, in which
case the present element is kept. Neither takes keywords ("set() takes no
keyword arguments") and each takes at most one argument ("set expected at most
1 argument, got *n*"). Whether `frozenset()` or `frozenset(f)` for an exact
frozenset *f* returns an existing object is implementation-defined (Annex D).

#### 26.3.2 Operations

For sets *a* and *b* (either type), the operators require both operands to be
sets or frozensets (otherwise they return `NotImplemented`); the corresponding
methods accept any iterables, several for the variadic ones:

| Operator | Method | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `a \| b` | `union(*others)` | the elements of either, in the type of *a* |
| `a & b` | `intersection(*others)` | the elements of both |
| `a - b` | `difference(*others)` | the elements of *a* not in *b* |
| `a ^ b` | `symmetric_difference(other)` | the elements of exactly one |
| `a <= b`, `a < b` | `issubset(other)` | every element of *a* is in *b* (and *a* ≠ *b*) |
| `a >= b`, `a > b` | `issuperset(other)` | the reverse |
| `a == b` | | the same elements, whatever the two types |
| | `isdisjoint(other)` | no common element |

For a `set` *a*, `|=`, `&=`, `-=`, `^=` update *a* in place (requiring a set
operand), as do `update`, `intersection_update`, `difference_update`, and
`symmetric_difference_update` (accepting iterables). `add(x)`, `remove(x)`
(`KeyError` *x* when absent), `discard(x)`, `pop()` (`KeyError` "pop from an
empty set"), `clear()`, and `copy()` complete the `set` methods. A `set` is
unhashable.

When an element argument of `in`, `remove`, or `discard` is a `set`, it is
looked up as a `frozenset` of the same elements.

`repr` of a set is "set()" when empty and otherwise `{`, the reprs of the
elements in iteration order separated by `", "`, and `}`; of a frozenset,
"frozenset()" or "frozenset(*r*)" with *r* the braces form; a subclass uses
its own name in place of `set` and `frozenset`.

#### 26.3.3 Mutation during iteration

A set iterator raises a `RuntimeError` exception ("Set changed size during
iteration") when the set's size changed since the iterator was created.

#### 26.3.4 FrozenSetHash ( *s* )

With unsigned 64-bit wrapping arithmetic and *shuffle*(*h*) = ((*h* XOR
89869747) XOR (*h* << 16)) × 3644798167:

1. Let *acc* be 0. For each element *x*, set *acc* to *acc* XOR
   *shuffle*(? Hash(*x*)).
2. Set *acc* to *acc* XOR ((len(*s*) + 1) × 1927868237).
3. Set *acc* to *acc* XOR (*acc* >> 11) XOR (*acc* >> 25), then to *acc* ×
   69069 + 907133923.
4. If *acc*, as a signed value, is −1, return 590923713; otherwise return it
   as a signed value.

The result does not depend on the iteration order.

### 26.4 Dictionary views

`d.keys()`, `d.values()`, and `d.items()` return *views* of `dict` or
`frozendict` *d*: they reflect later changes to *d*. Each has `len`, iteration
(in the order of *d*, subject to 26.1.4), `reversed`, membership (`(k, v) in
items` when *k* is present with a value equal to *v*), and a read-only
`mapping` attribute, a %MappingProxyType% of *d*. `repr` is
"dict_keys(*r*)", "dict_values(*r*)", "dict_items(*r*)" with *r* the list repr
of the elements.

Keys views and items views are *set-like*: `==`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` compare
them with any set or set-like view as sets, `isdisjoint` takes any iterable,
and `&`, `|`, `-`, `^` take any iterable operand and return a `set`. A values
view supports none of these (its `==` is identity).

### 26.5 Mappings

A *mapping* is an object whose `keys` method returns an iterable of keys and
whose `__getitem__` returns the value for each; DictMerge (13.2.3) and the
`**` forms accept any mapping in this sense. For `match` patterns (15.6.3, 15.6.4) the
built-in mappings are `dict`, `frozendict`, and %MappingProxyType%; another
class is treated as a mapping or a sequence only when it inherits from one of
the built-in ones or the host designates it (Annex C), a designation its
subclasses inherit.

### 26.6 The %MappingProxyType% intrinsic

%MappingProxyType% (`__name__` `"mappingproxy"`, module `builtins`) is a
read-only view of a mapping. `mappingproxy(mapping)` requires a mapping that is
not a `list` or `tuple` ("mappingproxy() argument must be a mapping, not
*tname*"). It provides `[]`, `in`, `len`, iteration, `reversed`, `get`,
`keys`, `values`, `items`, `copy` (delegating to the mapping's `copy`), `|`
(returning a new mapping from the underlying one), `==` and `hash` delegating
to the mapping, and `repr` "mappingproxy(*r*)". Item assignment and deletion
raise `TypeError` ("'mappingproxy' object does not support item assignment" /
"'mappingproxy' object doesn't support item deletion"), and `|=` raises
`TypeError` ("'|=' is not supported by mappingproxy; use '|' instead"). It is
not an acceptable base type. A program reaches it as `type(type.__dict__)`;
Annex C describes the library name `types.MappingProxyType`.

## 27 Control Abstraction Objects

### 27.1 Iteration

#### 27.1.1 Iterators

An *iterator* is an object whose type defines `__next__`, which returns the
next item or raises `StopIteration`. Every built-in iterator's `__iter__`
returns the iterator itself, and once it has raised `StopIteration` it raises
it on every later call (it is *exhausted*, and releases its source). Table
27-1 lists the intrinsic iterator types; none is an acceptable base type, and
each has `__length_hint__` where the length is known, and `__reduce__` and
`__setstate__` for the library facade (Annex C).

Table 27-1: Intrinsic iterator types

| `__name__` | Produced by |
| --- | --- |
| `iterator` | the sequence protocol (27.1.2) |
| `callable_iterator` | `iter(callable, sentinel)` (27.1.3) |
| `list_iterator`, `list_reverseiterator` | `iter` and `reversed` of a `list` |
| `tuple_iterator`, `range_iterator`, `bytes_iterator`, `bytearray_iterator` | `iter` (and for ranges `reversed`) of those types |
| `str_ascii_iterator`, `str_iterator` | `iter` of a `str` (the name depends on whether the string is ASCII and is implementation-defined, Annex D) |
| `dict_keyiterator`, `dict_valueiterator`, `dict_itemiterator`, and their `dict_reverse…` forms | `iter` and `reversed` of a dict or view |
| `set_iterator` | `iter` of a set |
| `enumerate`, `filter`, `map`, `reversed`, `zip` | the classes of 27.1.4–27.1.8, which are themselves iterators and acceptable base types |

#### 27.1.2 Sequence iterators

A sequence iterator over *o* holds *o* and an index *i*, initially 0. Its
`__next__`:

1. If the iterator is exhausted, raise `StopIteration`.
2. Let *r* be ? GetItem(*o*, *i*).
3. If *r* is a raise completion with an `IndexError` or `StopIteration`
   exception, mark the iterator exhausted and raise `StopIteration`. Other
   exceptions propagate.
4. Set *i* to *i* + 1 and return *r*.

#### 27.1.3 Callable iterators

`iter(callable, sentinel)` requires a callable ("iter(v, w): v must be
callable") and returns a callable iterator. Its `__next__` calls *callable*
with no arguments; if the call raises `StopIteration`, or the result *r* is
*sentinel* or ? RichCompare(*r*, *sentinel*, `==`) is true, it is exhausted
and raises `StopIteration`; otherwise it returns *r*.

#### 27.1.4 enumerate ( *iterable*, *start*=0 )

Returns an iterator yielding (*count*, *item*) pairs for the items of
? GetIterator(*iterable*), *count* starting at ToIndex(*start*) and increasing
by 1 ("enumerate() missing required argument 'iterable'").

#### 27.1.5 filter ( *function*, *iterable* )

Exactly two arguments ("filter expected 2 arguments, got *n*"). Yields the
items *x* of the iterable for which ToBoolean(? Call(*function*, «*x*»)) is
true, or ToBoolean(*x*) when *function* is `None`.

#### 27.1.6 map ( *function*, *iterable*, ...*iterables*, *strict*=False )

At least two arguments ("map() must have at least two arguments."). Each step
takes the next item of every iterable, left to right, and yields ? Call of
*function* with them; it stops when any iterable is exhausted. With *strict*
true, an iterable that ends while another does not raises the `ValueError` of
27.1.8 with "map()" in place of "zip()".

#### 27.1.7 reversed ( *seq* )

If LookupSpecial(*seq*, `"__reversed__"`) is not **absent** (and not `None`),
return ? Call of it. Otherwise, if *seq* has `__len__` and `__getitem__` (and
is not a dict), return an iterator yielding *seq*[*n* − 1], …, *seq*[0], with
*n* = ? Length(*seq*), stopping early at an `IndexError` or `StopIteration`.
Otherwise raise a `TypeError` exception ("'*tname*(*seq*)' object is not
reversible").

#### 27.1.8 zip ( ...*iterables*, *strict*=False )

Each step takes the next item of every iterator, left to right, and yields
their tuple; with no iterables it is immediately exhausted. When an iterable
*k* (1-based) is exhausted:

1. Without *strict*, stop.
2. With *strict*, if *k* > 1, raise a `ValueError` exception ("zip() argument
   *k* is shorter than argument 1" or "… than arguments 1-*j*" when *k* − 1 =
   *j* > 1). If *k* = 1, check each later iterable *m*: if it yields an item,
   raise a `ValueError` exception ("zip() argument *m* is longer than argument
   1" or "… than arguments 1-*j*"); if all are exhausted, stop.

### 27.2 Generator objects

A *generator object* is created by calling a generator function (16.1) or by
evaluating a generator expression (13.2.6); it owns a frame and a *state*:
**created**, **suspended**, **running**, or **closed**. Coroutines (27.4) are
generator-like objects with the same machinery and the state names apply to
them too.

#### 27.2.1 GeneratorResume ( *g*, *mode*, *value* )

The abstract operation GeneratorResume resumes *g* with *mode* **send**
(delivering *value*), **throw** (delivering the exception *value*), or
**close**. *kind* is "generator", "coroutine", or "async generator".

1. If *g*'s state is **running**, raise a `ValueError` exception ("*kind*
   already executing").
2. If the state is **closed**: for **send**, raise `StopIteration` (for a
   coroutine, a `RuntimeError` exception "cannot reuse already awaited
   coroutine"); for **throw**, raise *value*; for **close**,
   return `None`.
3. If the state is **created**: for **send** with *value* not `None`, raise a
   `TypeError` exception ("can't send non-None value to a just-started
   *kind*"); for **throw**, set the state to **closed** and raise *value*; for
   **close**, set the state to **closed** and return `None`.
4. Set the state to **running** and resume the frame: in **created** state at
   the start of the body; otherwise at the suspended `yield` (or
   `yield from`/`await`), which receives *value* or the exception as 13.16.1
   and 13.16.3 specify (for **close**, a new `GeneratorExit`).
5. If the frame suspends with *v*, set the state to **suspended** and return
   *v*.
6. If the frame completes, set the state to **closed**, release the frame, and:
   1. For a return completion with value *v*, raise a new `StopIteration`
      whose `value` is *v* (with no argument when *v* is `None`).
   2. For a raise completion with a `StopIteration` exception *s*, raise a new
      `RuntimeError` exception ("*kind* raised StopIteration") whose
      `__cause__` is *s* (for an async generator, likewise for
      `StopAsyncIteration`).
   3. Otherwise propagate the completion.

#### 27.2.2 Methods

- `__next__()` is GeneratorResume(*g*, **send**, `None`).
- `send(value)` is GeneratorResume(*g*, **send**, *value*).
- `throw(value)`: *value* shall be an exception instance or class
  ("exceptions must be classes or instances deriving from BaseException, not
  *tname*"); a class is instantiated. The deprecated forms `throw(type,
  value[, tb])` normalize *value* to an instance of *type* (an instance with a
  separate value raises `TypeError` "instance exception may not have a
  separate value"; a non-traceback *tb* "throw() third argument must be a
  traceback object") and issue a `DeprecationWarning`. Then
  GeneratorResume(*g*, **throw**, the exception).
- `close()`: let *r* be GeneratorResume(*g*, **close**, —). If it raised
  `GeneratorExit` or `StopIteration` (with value *v*), return `None` (or *v*,
  the value of a `return` in the generator). If it returned a value, the
  generator yielded: raise a `RuntimeError` exception ("*kind* ignored
  GeneratorExit"). Otherwise propagate.
- `__iter__()` returns *g* (generators only).

#### 27.2.3 Attributes

| Attribute | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `__name__`, `__qualname__` | from the function; writable, requiring a `str` ("\_\_name\_\_ must be set to a string object", "\_\_qualname\_\_ must be set to a string object") |
| `gi_frame` | the frame, or `None` once closed |
| `gi_running`, `gi_suspended` | whether the state is **running**, **suspended** (read-only) |
| `gi_code` | the code object |
| `gi_yieldfrom` | the iterator a suspended `yield from` is delegating to, or `None` |

`repr(g)` is "\<generator object *qualname* at *address*\>".

#### 27.2.4 Finalization

When a generator, coroutine, or asynchronous generator in **suspended** state
becomes unreachable, the implementation calls its `close()` (for an
asynchronous generator, the host finalizer of 27.5.4) before reclaiming it. An
exception from that call is passed to HostReportUnraisable. A coroutine that
is reclaimed in **created** state causes a `RuntimeWarning` ("coroutine
'*qualname*' was never awaited") to be issued.

### 27.3 Awaitables

An *awaitable* is a coroutine, an iterable coroutine (27.4.5), or an object
whose type defines `__await__` returning an iterator (GetAwaitable, 7.7.5).
`await` delegates to that iterator with YieldFrom (13.16.3): the values it
yields pass out to the code driving the outermost coroutine, and the value of
its `StopIteration` is the result of the `await`.

### 27.4 Coroutine objects

#### 27.4.1 Coroutines

Calling a coroutine function (`async def`) creates a *coroutine object* in
**created** state; its body runs only when it is driven. Coroutines are not
iterable ("'coroutine' object is not iterable") and have no `__next__`.

#### 27.4.2 Methods

`send`, `throw`, and `close` are those of 27.2.2 with *kind* "coroutine".
`__await__()` returns a *coroutine wrapper* (`__name__`
`"coroutine_wrapper"`), an iterator whose `__next__`, `send`, `throw`, and
`close` operate on the coroutine.

#### 27.4.3 Awaiting once

A coroutine may be awaited only once: GetAwaitable of a coroutine whose state
is **closed** after running, or that is already being awaited, raises a
`RuntimeError` exception ("cannot reuse already awaited coroutine"; "coroutine
is being awaited already").

#### 27.4.4 Attributes

`cr_frame`, `cr_running`, `cr_suspended`, `cr_code`, and `cr_await` (the
awaitable being awaited, or `None`) correspond to 27.2.3; `cr_origin` is
`None` unless the host tracks origins. `repr` is "\<coroutine object
*qualname* at *address*\>".

#### 27.4.5 Iterable coroutines

A generator function whose code carries the *iterable coroutine* flag (set by
the host's facade, Annex C: `types.coroutine`) creates generators that
GetAwaitable accepts directly and that may `yield from` a coroutine.

### 27.5 Asynchronous generator objects

Calling an asynchronous generator function creates an *asynchronous generator
object* (`async_generator`). It is not iterable ("'async_generator' object is
not iterable"); its `__aiter__` returns itself.

#### 27.5.1 Asynchronous generator awaitables

`__anext__()` and `asend(value)` return a new `async_generator_asend` object,
and `athrow(…)` and `aclose()` a new `async_generator_athrow` object. Each is an
awaitable whose iterator drives the generator once:

1. Driving one of these awaitables a second time after it finished, or after
   `close()`, raises a `RuntimeError` exception ("cannot reuse already awaited
   \_\_anext\_\_()/asend()" or "cannot reuse already awaited
   aclose()/athrow()").
2. If another such awaitable of the same generator is in progress, raise a
   `RuntimeError` exception ("anext(): asynchronous generator is already
   running", or "athrow(): …", "aclose(): …").
3. Resume the generator with GeneratorResume (kind "async generator"),
   passing *value* (`None` for `__anext__`) on the first step, and passing
   through the values that `await` expressions in the body yield, to the
   driver.
4. When the body executes `yield v`, the awaitable finishes: it raises
   `StopIteration` with value *v*.
5. When the body finishes normally, the awaitable raises
   `StopAsyncIteration`.

#### 27.5.2 athrow and aclose

- `athrow(value)` throws *value* into the generator at the suspended `yield`.
  If the generator is finished, the awaitable completes with `None`; if
  `aclose()` was called and the generator was not finished, it raises
  `StopAsyncIteration` without running the body. Thrown into a generator in
  **created** state, the exception is raised and the generator is closed.
- `aclose()` throws `GeneratorExit`. If the body handles it by finishing or
  re-raising, the awaitable completes with `None`; if the body yields again,
  the awaitable raises a `RuntimeError` exception ("async generator ignored
  GeneratorExit"). After the first `aclose()`, later `aclose()` and `athrow()`
  awaitables raise `StopAsyncIteration` until the generator finishes, and
  complete with `None` afterwards; `asend()` still resumes the body.

#### 27.5.3 Attributes

`ag_frame`, `ag_running`, `ag_suspended`, `ag_code`, `ag_await`,
`__name__`, and `__qualname__` correspond to 27.2.3. `repr` is
"\<async_generator object *qualname* at *address*\>".

#### 27.5.4 Host hooks

On the first iteration of an asynchronous generator the implementation calls
the host's *firstiter* hook, and when a suspended one becomes unreachable it
calls the host's *finalizer* hook with the generator, if the host installed
them (HostAsyncGenHooks, Annex B); without a finalizer it calls `aclose()` and
drives the resulting awaitable to completion, reporting any exception as
unraisable.

## 28 Reflection Objects

The objects of this clause describe running code. Each type is an intrinsic
that is not an acceptable base type, with `__module__` `"builtins"`; a program
reaches it through `type()` of an instance, and Annex C lists the names the
library facade gives them.

### 28.1 Function type

%FunctionType% (`__name__` `"function"`) is the type of user-defined functions
(16.2). Calling it, `function(code, globals, name=None, argdefs=None,
closure=None, kwdefaults=None)`, creates a function directly:

1. *code* shall be a code object ("function() argument 'code' must be code,
   not *tname*") and *globals* a `dict` ("function() argument 'globals' must be
   dict, not *tname*").
